22. Pronunciation rules and a stratified sample of the rule-generated respellings (not the 6,630 derived rows)

Priority 5 · 656 items · 7.7 h estimated · suggested reviewer: Hebrew teacher; cantor for Sephardi and Ashkenazi
How to use this printout.
  • Read the Hebrew aloud. Tick ok only if you would put your name to it; fix if you can give the right text; unsure if you cannot tell.
  • The record of your answers is the matching CSV sheet (same ids), not this page. Use this page for reading and for notes on paper.
  • Items are in the order of the sheet. Please do not guess: "unsure" is a useful answer.
pron-rules:rule:sign:alefP5 · flagged: derived
אֶל
israeli: el | sephardi: el | ashkenazi: el
Rule sign:alef (sign): alef
alef: Israeli ∅; Sephardi ʔ (when it carries a vowel); Ashkenazi ∅. Source and notes: consonants.json (Israeli "[ʔ] or ∅": written ∅; a hiatus shows as a syllable break)
Records using it: israeli 1289, sephardi 1289, ashkenazi 1289.

pron-rules:rule:sign:heP5 · flagged: derived
נָהַג
israeli: nahag | sephardi: nahag | ashkenazi: nohag
Rule sign:he (sign): he
he: Israeli h; silent when it is a final vowel letter; mappiq = h; Sephardi same; Ashkenazi same. Source and notes: hebrew.js / syllabify.js. A final he + patach **without** mappiq (גָּבֹהַ in the source) is read as mappiq + furtive patach and flagged `he-patach-no-mappiq`
Records using it: israeli 2085, sephardi 2085, ashkenazi 2085.
Check: Rule sign:he. Is this the right rule for the tradition(s) named, in how its sound is spelled and where the stress falls? Say which tradition or community you judge by. If the rule is wrong, say what you would say instead; the example words are only illustrations.

pron-rules:rule:sign:kamatz-katanP5 · flagged: derived
אׇמָּן
israeli: oman | sephardi: oman | ashkenazi: omon
Rule sign:kamatz-katan (sign): kamatz katan
kamatz katan: Israeli o; Sephardi ɔ; Ashkenazi ɔ. Source and notes: niqqud.json variant. Detected only from U+05C7, the four-word list, and a kamatz immediately before a chataf-kamatz (צָהֳרַיִם; **own rule**, 2 hits). Any other kamatz in a closed unstressed syllable is read as gadol and is **not** detected (known gap, e.g. חָפְשִׁי)
Records using it: israeli 30, sephardi 30, ashkenazi 30.
Check: Rule sign:kamatz-katan. Is this the right rule for the tradition(s) named, in how its sound is spelled and where the stress falls? Say which tradition or community you judge by. If the rule is wrong, say what you would say instead; the example words are only illustrations.

pron-rules:rule:sign:shvaP5 · flagged: derived
זְבֻל
סְפָר
israeli: zevul | sephardi: zevul | ashkenazi: zevul
israeli: sefar | sephardi: sefar | ashkenazi: sefor
Rule sign:shva (sign): shva na / shva nach
shva na: Israeli e; Sephardi e; Ashkenazi ə. Source and notes: niqqud.json (Ashkenazi "[e]/[ə] when read strictly"). Israeli value is the careful/liturgical reading; **colloquial Israeli drops many of these** (zman, kmo). The `words.json` ipa field shows the colloquial form for 178 words; those are flagged `words-ipa-colloquial-shva-drop`, not changed
shva nach: Israeli ∅; Sephardi ∅; Ashkenazi ∅. Source and notes: shva.js rules. 176 records depend on the L3 "after a long unaccented vowel" heuristic (flag `shva-heuristic-L3`)
Records using it: israeli 2636, sephardi 2636, ashkenazi 2636.
Check: Rule sign:shva. Is this the right rule for the tradition(s) named, in how its sound is spelled and where the stress falls? Say which tradition or community you judge by. If the rule is wrong, say what you would say instead; the example words are only illustrations.
ok fix unsure

pron-rules:rule:sign:tzereP5 · flagged: derived
מֵת
israeli: met | sephardi: met | ashkenazi: meis
Rule sign:tzere (sign): tzere
tzere: Israeli e; Sephardi e; Ashkenazi ej. Source and notes: niqqud.json; *Ashkenazi Hebrew*: "[ej] in the majority of Ashkenazic traditions"; *Sephardi Hebrew*: "[e(ː)], not [ei]". tzere + yod is the same vowel (the yod is a vowel letter); `simple` writes "ei" in all three (siddur convention of the transliterator)
Records using it: israeli 1368, sephardi 1368, ashkenazi 1368.
Check: Rule sign:tzere. Is this the right rule for the tradition(s) named, in how its sound is spelled and where the stress falls? Say which tradition or community you judge by. If the rule is wrong, say what you would say instead; the example words are only illustrations.

pron-rules:rule:sign:vavP5 · flagged: derived
זוּם
israeli: zum | sephardi: zum | ashkenazi: zum
Rule sign:vav (sign): vav
vav: Israeli v; vowel letter for cholam/shuruk; conjunction וּ = u; Sephardi same; Ashkenazi same. Source and notes: syllabify.js. A vav-cholam or shuruk after a **silent alef** (מְאוֹד, בְּאוּר) is repaired to a vowel letter (syllabify reads it as a consonant; transliterate.js already reads it right), flag `alef-vav-mater-repaired`
Records using it: israeli 2015, sephardi 2015, ashkenazi 2015.
Check: Rule sign:vav. Is this the right rule for the tradition(s) named, in how its sound is spelled and where the stress falls? Say which tradition or community you judge by. If the rule is wrong, say what you would say instead; the example words are only illustrations.

Blessed is the name of the Master of the world; blessed is your crown and your place. May your good will be with your people Israel for ever; show your people the redemption of your right hand in your Temple, bring us of the goodness of your light, and accept our prayer in mercy. May it be your will to lengthen our life in goodness, and let me be counted among the righteous, so that you have mercy on me and guard me and all that is mine and your people Israel's. You feed and sustain all; you rule over all; you rule over kings, and the kingdom is yours. I am a servant of the Holy One, blessed be he, before whom and before whose glorious Torah I bow at every time. Not in any person do I trust, and not on any angel do I rely, but on the God of heaven, who is the God of truth, whose Torah is truth and whose prophets are truth, and who abounds in deeds of goodness and truth. In him I trust, and to his holy and glorious name I utter praises. May it be your will to open my heart to Torah, to fulfil the requests of my heart and the heart of all your people Israel, for good, for life and for peace.

Blessed is the name of the Master of the world; blessed is your crown and your place. May your good will be with your people Israel for ever; show your people the redemption of your right hand in your Temple, bring us of the goodness of your light, and accept our prayer in mercy. May it be your will to lengthen our life in goodness, and let me be counted among the righteous, so that you have mercy on me and guard me and all that is mine and your people Israel's. You feed and sustain all; you rule over all; you rule over kings, and the kingdom is yours. I am a servant of the Holy One, blessed be he, before whom and before whose glorious Torah I bow at every time. Not in any person do I trust, and not on any angel do I rely, but on the God of heaven, who is the God of truth, whose Torah is truth and whose prophets are truth, and who abounds in deeds of goodness and truth. In him I trust, and to his holy and glorious name I utter praises. May it be your will to open my heart to Torah, to fulfil the requests of my heart and the heart of all your people Israel, for good, for life and for peace.

Blessed is the name of the Master of the world; blessed is your crown and your place. May your good will be with your people Israel for ever; show your people the redemption of your right hand in your Temple, bring us of the goodness of your light, and accept our prayer in mercy. May it be your will to lengthen our life in goodness, and let me be counted among the righteous, so that you have mercy on me and guard me and all that is mine and your people Israel's. You feed and sustain all; you rule over all; you rule over kings, and the kingdom is yours. I am a servant of the Holy One, blessed be he, before whom and before whose glorious Torah I bow at every time. Not in any person do I trust, and not on any angel do I rely, but on the God of heaven, who is the God of truth, whose Torah is truth and whose prophets are truth, and who abounds in deeds of goodness and truth. Kamatz (ָ)
nav: Kamatz
summary: The T-shaped vowel sign under a letter. Israeli and Sephardi readers say [a], like the a in "father". The Ashkenazi reading says [ɔ], like the o in "saw", so בָּרוּךְ is barukh in one and borukh in the other.
rows.0[0]: israeli
rows.0[1]: [a]
rows.0[2]: ba-RUKH
rows.1[0]: sephardi
rows.1[1]: [a]
rows.1[2]: ba-RUKH
rows.2[0]: ashkenazi
rows.2[1]: [ɔ]
rows.2[2]: BO-rukh
who: Rule-generated: the [a] reading is the one used here for Israeli and Sephardi, the [ɔ] reading for Ashkenazi (the rule is "kamatz gadol is generally [ɔ]" in the cited description of Ashkenazi Hebrew). Not checked by a native speaker.
limits: A few words use a kamatz that is read as a short o in every tradition (kamatz katan, as in כָּל "kol"). Only a short list of such words is recognised here, so other words of that kind are read wrongly.
why: Kamatz (ָ): a in Israeli and Sephardi, but [ɔ] ("o" as in "saw") in Ashkenazi.

examples.5: מֵת
Tav without a dot (ת)
nav: Tav
summary: A tav with no dagesh dot is t in the Israeli and Sephardi readings. In the Ashkenazi reading it is s, which is why שַׁבָּת is shabat in one and shabos in the other. A tav with a dot is t in all three.
rows.0[0]: israeli
rows.0[1]: t
rows.0[2]: shabat
rows.1[0]: sephardi
rows.1[1]: t
rows.1[2]: shabat
rows.2[0]: ashkenazi
rows.2[1]: s
rows.2[2]: shabos
who: Rule-generated: Ashkenazi s for a tav without dagesh, as in the cited description ("ungeminated tav is pronounced [s]"). Some Sephardi communities say th or d here; those are not generated.
limits: The rule needs the dagesh to be written in the pointed text. A word with the dot missing is read with s in the Ashkenazi column.
why: Tav without a dagesh (ת): t in Israeli and Sephardi, but s in Ashkenazi (shabat / shabos).
examples.0.gloss: used to introduce a semantically definit
examples.0.ipa[0]: ˈet

examples.0.simple[1]: eis

examples.1.simple[1]: bayis

Holam (ֹ)
nav: Holam
summary: The dot above a letter. Israeli and Sephardi readers say a plain o. The Ashkenazi reading used here says "oy" [oj], the Polish and Galician value, so תּוֹרָה is torah in one and toyreh in the other.
rows.0[0]: israeli
rows.0[1]: [o]
rows.0[2]: to-RA
rows.1[0]: sephardi
rows.1[1]: [o]
rows.1[2]: to-RA
rows.2[0]: ashkenazi
rows.2[1]: [oj]
rows.2[2]: TOY-reh
who: Rule-generated: one Ashkenazi value is chosen. Other Ashkenazi communities say o, ey or au; they are not shown. Not checked by a native speaker.
limits: Only the Polish-style value is generated, so a reader from another Ashkenazi tradition will disagree with the Ashkenazi column.
why: Cholam (ֹ): o in Israeli and Sephardi, but "oy" [oj] in the Polish-style Ashkenazi reading used here (other Ashkenazi readings differ and are not shown).

Het (ח)
nav: Het
summary: Israeli readers say [χ], a rasp at the back of the mouth, the same as khaf. The Sephardi reading used here is a deeper throat sound [ħ]. The Ashkenazi reading is [x], like khaf.
rows.0[0]: israeli
rows.0[1]: [χ]
rows.0[2]: like kh in "Bach"
rows.1[0]: sephardi
rows.1[1]: [ħ]
rows.1[2]: breathy, from the throat
rows.2[0]: ashkenazi
rows.2[1]: [x]
rows.2[2]: like kh in "Bach"
who: Rule-generated from the cited tables. In practice many Ashkenazi and Sephardi readers say het and khaf alike, and some Sephardi and Mizrahi readers keep the throat sound.
limits: The difference between [χ] and [x] is too small to hear in most voices; the [ħ] is the audible one.
why: Het (ח): [χ] in Israeli, a deeper throat sound [ħ] in Sephardi, [x] in Ashkenazi.

nav: Ayin
summary: Israeli and Ashkenazi readers say nothing for an ayin (it behaves like alef). The Sephardi reading used here keeps a throat sound [ʕ], a squeezed "ah" from deep in the throat.

rows.0[2]: no sound
rows.1[0]: sephardi
rows.1[1]: [ʕ]
rows.1[2]: squeezed throat sound
rows.2[0]: ashkenazi
rows.2[1]: (silent)
rows.2[2]: no sound
who: Rule-generated: [ʕ] for Sephardi follows the cited description. Spanish-Portuguese communities use a different sound [ŋ] that is not generated.
limits: A silent ayin still splits the syllables, which the spelled-out respelling shows as an apostrophe.
why: Ayin (ע): silent in Israeli and Ashkenazi, a throat sound [ʕ] in Sephardi.

Resh (ר)
nav: Resh
summary: Israeli readers say a back-of-the-throat [ʁ], like the French r. The Sephardi and Ashkenazi readings used here say a front r, rolled or tapped like the Spanish r.
rows.0[0]: israeli
rows.0[1]: [ʁ]
rows.0[2]: French-style r
rows.1[0]: sephardi
rows.1[1]: [r]
rows.1[2]: rolled or tapped
rows.2[0]: ashkenazi
rows.2[1]: [r]
rows.2[2]: rolled or tapped
who: Rule-generated: the Sephardi value follows the cited description. The Ashkenazi value is chosen from "alveolar flap or trill, or uvular by region"; a uvular Ashkenazi reader will disagree.
limits: The respelling writes r for all three, so only the IPA line shows this difference.
why: Resh (ר): a back-of-the-throat [ʁ] in Israeli, a front rolled or tapped r in Sephardi and Ashkenazi.

Which syllable is stressed
nav: Stress
summary: Israeli and Sephardi readers stress the last syllable of most words (שַׁבָּת is sha-BAT). The Ashkenazi reading stresses the next-to-last syllable (SHA-bes). Some words are stressed earlier in every tradition, such as מֶלֶךְ (ME-lekh).
rows.0[0]: israeli
rows.0[1]: last syllable
rows.0[2]: sha-BAT
rows.1[0]: sephardi
rows.1[1]: last syllable
rows.1[2]: sha-BAT
rows.2[0]: ashkenazi
rows.2[1]: next-to-last
rows.2[2]: SHA-bes
who: Rule-generated: last-syllable stress is the default and exceptions come only from short existing lists; next-to-last stress is the default for Ashkenazi. Not checked by a native speaker.
limits: About 240 words that look like two-syllable nouns ending in segol are read with last-syllable stress by default and may be wrong. Prefixed words (וּבְכָל) get an Ashkenazi stress a trained reader would not give.
why:
